قابل‌اعتماد دستورالعمل‌های قابل‌اعتماد را از دادهٔ نامعتبر در همان جریان زمینه تشخیص دهند. فروشندگان می‌توانند زنجیره‌های اکسپلویت خاص را وصله کنند، همان‌طور که مایکروسافت با EchoLeak انجام داد، اما مصنوعی‌ای را که از طرف شما محتوای خارجی می‌خواند تحت‌تأثیر قرار می‌دهد، شامل توسعه‌دهندگان فردی که از دستیارهای کدنویسی هوش مصنوعی، اکستنشن‌های مرورگر، یا ابزارهای متصل به MCP استفاده می‌کنند. ریسک با آن‌چه عامل می‌تواند بخواند و آن‌چه اجازه دارد انجام دهد مقیاس می‌شود، نه با اندازهٔ سازمانی که آن را استفاده می‌کند."}},{"@type":"Question","name":"کدام نوع عامل‌های هوش مصنوعی بیشترین ریسک را 1a:["$","$L24","fa",{"locale":"fa"}]
26:T535,The attack chain, as documented by Aim Labs and later analyzed in an academic case study, worked in stages. An attacker sent an ordinary-looking email containing a hidden instruction, embedded either in an HTML comment or as white text on a white background, invisible to a human reader but fully readable to Copilot's retrieval system. The victim never needed to open or interact with that email specifically — they just needed to later ask Copilot an unrelated question, at which point its retrieval-augmented generation system pulled the earlier email back into context as potentially relevant background. The hidden instruction rode along, and Copilot followed it. To actually get data out, the attackers chained several additional bypasses: they evaded Microsoft's cross-prompt injection classifier with specific phrasing, got around link redaction using reference-style Markdown formatting, exploited the fact that Copilot auto-fetches images, and abused a Microsoft Teams proxy that the product's own content security policy happened to allow. The final step had Copilot generate a response containing an image reference pointing to an attacker-controlled server, with the stolen data encoded into the URL itself — so the moment the image tried to load, the data left the building, silently, with no click and no download.27:T4d7,In March 2026, a backdoored version of LiteLLM — the language-model gateway that frameworks including CrewAI, DSPy, and Microsoft's GraphRAG depend on — sat live on PyPI for roughly three hours and was pulled nearly 47,000 times before it was caught. Anyone who updated during that window pulled in an autonomous attack tool that researchers nicknamed hackerbot-claw alongside their normal dependency update. That same bot had, the month before, exploited misconfigured GitHub Actions workflows across open-source repositories on its own, then harvested a publishing token through a compromised CI setup at a security vendor and used it to push the backdoored LiteLLM packages directly to PyPI — a full attack chain executed without a human directing each step. OWASP's GenAI Security Project has been tracking incidents like this closely enough that its 2026 State of Agentic AI Security report reads, in its own words, very differently from the 2025 edition: where the earlier report cataloged plausible future threats, the current one catalogs actual CVEs, vendor advisories, and confirmed breach reports across nearly every category of agentic risk, with coding agents specifically driving the largest share of new incident data.28:T4eb,Microsoft's cross-prompt injection classifier, the system EchoLeak was specifically engineered to slip past, is a reasonable example of the current state of the art: a dedicated model trained to spot instruction-like text hidden inside content the AI is supposed to treat as data. It works against unsophisticated attacks. It does not work against an attacker who iterates against it, because the classifier itself is just another model making a probabilistic judgment call, and probabilistic judgment calls have edge cases by definition. The Five Eyes advisory referenced earlier put this bluntly: adaptive attacks bypass essentially every published defense, which is a genuinely unusual thing for a government security advisory to state about an entire category of mitigation rather than a single flawed product. Security researchers testing layered defenses — combining an input classifier, output filtering, and strict permission boundaries together rather than relying on any single layer — have measured meaningfully better results, with attack success rates dropping from roughly 73% down to under 9% in controlled evaluations. That's real progress, but it's also an explicit admission that no single defense gets anywhere close to zero on its own.29:T919,زنجیرهٔ حمله، همان‌طور که توسط Aim Labs مستند شد و بعداً در یک مطالعهٔ موردی آکادمیک تحلیل شد، در مراحلی کار کرد. یک مهاجم ایمیلی معمولی به نظر می‌رسید فرستاد که حاوی یک دستورالعمل پنهان بود، یا درون یک کامنت HTML یا به‌شکل متن سفید روی پس‌زمینهٔ سفید تعبیه‌شده بود، نامرئی برای یک خوانندهٔ انسانی اما کاملاً خوانا برای سیستم بازیابی Copilot. قربانی هرگز نیازی نداشت آن ایمیل خاص را باز کند یا با آن تعامل کند — فقط لازم بود بعداً از Copilot یک سؤال بی‌ربط بپرسد، که در آن نقطه سیستم تولید تقویت‌شده با بازیابی آن، ایمیل قبلی را به‌عنوان زمینهٔ پس‌زمینهٔ بالقوه مرتبط به زمینه برگرداند. دستورالعمل پنهان همراه سوار شد، و Copilot از آن پیروی کرد. برای واقعاً بیرون‌کشیدن داده، مهاجمان چند دور زدن اضافی را زنجیر کردند: آن‌ها طبقه‌بند اینجکشن متقاطع پرامپت مایکروسافت را با عبارت‌پردازی خاص دور زدند، حذف لینک را با قالب‌بندی مارک‌داون سبک ارجاعی دور زدند، از این واقعیت که Copilot به‌طور خودکار تصاویر را واکشی می‌کند بهره‌برداری کردند، و از یک پروکسی Microsoft Teams سوءاستفاده کردند که خط‌مشی امنیت محتوای خود محصول اتفاقاً اجازه‌اش را می‌داد. گام نهایی باعث شد Copilot پاسخی حاوی یک ارجاع تصویر تولید کند که به سرور تحت کنترل مهاجم اشاره می‌کرد، با داده‌های سرقت‌شده کدگذاری‌شده درون خود URL — پس لحظه‌ای که تصویر سعی کرد بارگذاری شود، داده‌ها ساختمان را ترک کردند، بی‌صدا، بدون هیچ کلیک و دانلودی.2a:T83f,در مارس ۲۰۲۶، نسخهٔ درپشتی‌دار LiteLLM — دروازهٔ مدل زبانی‌ای که چارچوب‌هایی شامل CrewAI، DSPy و GraphRAG مایکروسافت به آن وابسته‌اند — حدود سه ساعت زنده روی PyPI نشست و نزدیک به ۴۷ هزار بار پیش از کشف‌شدن دانلود شد. هرکسی که در آن بازه به‌روزرسانی کرد، همراه با به‌روزرسانی وابستگی عادی خود، یک ابزار حملهٔ خودمختار که پژوهشگران آن را hackerbot-claw نام‌گذاری کردند دریافت کرد. همان بات، یک ماه پیش از آن، به‌تنهایی جریان‌های کاری پیکربندی‌نادرست GitHub Actions را در سراسر مخازن متن‌باز بهره‌برداری کرده بود، سپس یک توکن انتشار را از طریق یک راه‌اندازی CI به‌خطرافتاده در یک فروشندهٔ امنیتی برداشت کرد و از آن برای فشار مستقیم بسته‌های درپشتی‌دار LiteLLM به PyPI استفاده کرد — یک زنجیرهٔ حملهٔ کامل که بدون هدایت انسانی هر گام اجرا شد. پروژهٔ امنیت GenAI اوواسپ به‌اندازه‌ای دقیق رخدادهایی مانند این را ردیابی کرده که گزارش وضعیت امنیت هوش مصنوعی عاملی ۲۰۲۶ آن، به گفتهٔ خودش، بسیار متفاوت از نسخهٔ ۲۰۲۵ خوانده می‌شود: جایی که گزارش قبلی‌تر تهدیدهای آیندهٔ محتمل را فهرست می‌کرد، نسخهٔ فعلی CVEهای واقعی، اطلاعیه‌های فروشنده و گزارش‌های نقض تأییدشده را در تقریباً هر دستهٔ ریسک عاملی فهرست می‌کند، با عامل‌های کدنویسی که مشخصاً بزرگ‌ترین سهم داده‌های رخداد جدید را به دوش می‌کشند.2b:T806,طبقه‌بند اینجکشن متقاطع پرامپت مایکروسافت، سیستمی که EchoLeak مشخصاً برای لغزیدن از کنار آن مهندسی شده بود، نمونهٔ معقولی از وضعیت فعلی هنر است: مدلی اختصاصی آموزش‌دیده برای شناسایی متن شبیه‌دستورالعمل پنهان درون محتوایی که قرار است هوش مصنوعی آن را داده در نظر بگیرد. این علیه حملات ساده کار می‌کند. علیه مهاجمی که در برابر آن تکرار می‌کند کار نمی‌کند، چون خود طبقه‌بند فقط یک مدل دیگر است که یک قضاوت احتمالاتی می‌کند، و قضاوت‌های احتمالاتی طبق تعریف موارد لبه دارند. اطلاعیهٔ Five Eyes که پیش‌تر ذکر شد این را صریح بیان کرد: حملات تطبیقی اساساً از هر دفاع منتشرشده‌ای عبور می‌کنند، که چیز واقعاً غیرمعمولی برای یک اطلاعیهٔ امنیتی دولتی است تا دربارهٔ کل یک دستهٔ کاهش‌دهنده به‌جای یک محصول معیوب منفرد بیان کند. پژوهشگران امنیتی‌ای که دفاع‌های لایه‌ای را تست می‌کنند — ترکیب یک طبقه‌بند ورودی، فیلترکردن خروجی و مرزهای مجوز کمترین-امتیاز سخت‌گیرانه با هم به‌جای اتکا به هر لایهٔ منفرد — نتایج به‌طور معناداری بهتری اندازه‌گیری کرده‌اند، با نرخ موفقیت حمله که از حدود ۷۳٪ به زیر ۹٪ در ارزیابی‌های کنترل‌شده افت می‌کند. این پیشرفت واقعی است، اما همچنین اعترافی صریح است که هیچ دفاع منفردی به‌تنهایی نزدیک به صفر نمی‌رسد.1c:["$","$L25",null,{"locale":"fa","post":{"slug":"prompt-injection-is-the-bug-ai-companies-cant-fully-patch-should-you-worry","status":"published","title":"Prompt Injection Is the Bug AI Companies Can't Fully Patch. Should You Worry?","date":"2026-08-31 11:21:21","tags":["ai-coding-agents","ai-tools"],"featuredImage":{"src":"/api/uploads/20260831-111200-74d45cc8a23445a5.webp","alt":{"en":"Prompt Injection Is the Bug AI Companies Can't Fully Patch. Should You Worry?","fa":"پرامپت اینجکشن باگی است که شرکت‌های هوش مصنوعی نمی‌توانند کاملاً وصله کنند. باید نگران باشید؟"}},"translations":{"en":{"title":"Prompt Injection Is the Bug AI Companies Can't Fully Patch. Should You Worry?","excerpt":"In 2025, a single crafted email silently exfiltrated data from Microsoft 365 Copilot with zero clicks. In 2026, a supply-chain attack rewrote itself into an autonomous bot with no human involved. Prompt injection isn't a bug you patch once and forget — here's what it actually is, why it keeps working, and what genuinely reduces your risk.","content":[{"type":"heading","level":1,"text":"Prompt Injection Is the Bug AI Companies Can't Fully Patch. Should You Worry?"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"In June 2025, security researchers at Aim Labs disclosed a vulnerability called EchoLeak, tracked as CVE-2025-32711 with a CVSS severity score of 9.3. A single email, sent to a target's inbox with no attachment and no link, was enough to silently extract sensitive internal data from Microsoft 365 Copilot. The victim never clicked anything. They didn't even need to open the email — they just needed to later ask Copilot an ordinary, unrelated question, and hidden instructions buried in white-on-white text inside that earlier email rode along with the AI's retrieved context and told it to leak data to an attacker's server."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"That's prompt injection, and it isn't a bug in the traditional sense — there's no single line of vulnerable code to patch, because the flaw lives in a structural property of how large language models work: they can't reliably tell the difference between an instruction from their operator and text they're merely supposed to read. Microsoft patched the specific EchoLeak exploit chain server-side, but the underlying category of risk didn't go away, and it hasn't gone away for any AI vendor. By mid-2026, security researchers had documented findings against Slack AI, Cursor, GitHub's MCP integration, and a growing list of AI coding assistants, and a joint advisory from the Five Eyes intelligence alliance stated plainly that current defenses don't solve the problem — adaptive attacks bypass essentially every published mitigation. So the honest question isn't whether prompt injection is real. It's how worried you personally need to be, based on what kind of AI tools you actually use."},{"type":"stats","items":[{"value":"73%","label":"of AI systems assessed in security audits showed exposure to some form of prompt injection vulnerability (State of AI Security 2026)"},{"value":"9.3","label":"CVSS severity score assigned to EchoLeak (CVE-2025-32711), the first documented zero-click showing the three types of prompt injection attacks: direct injection typed by the attacker, indirect injection hidden in external content the AI reads, and stored injection persisted in memory or a database for later triggering","caption":"Figure 1: Direct, indirect, and stored prompt injection each exploit a different trust boundary — but all three rely on the same underlying weakness: AI models can't cleanly separate instructions from data.","altText":"Diagram showing the three types of prompt injection attacks: direct injection typed by the attacker, indirect injection hidden in external content the AI reads, and stored injection persisted in memory or a database for later triggering"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"What Prompt Injection Actually Is, in Plain Terms"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"A prompt injection attack manipulates an AI model or agent into following an attacker's instructions instead of its operator's, by hiding those instructions somewhere the model will read them as part of its normal task."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Security researchers generally split this into three categories, and the difference between them is where the malicious instruction enters the system. Direct prompt injection is the original, simplest form: the attacker types the malicious instruction straight into the prompt themselves, the way someone might try to get a chatbot to ignore its safety rules by typing 'ignore all previous instructions.' Indirect prompt injection is more dangerous precisely because the attacker never touches the AI directly — instead they plant instructions somewhere the AI is later going to read on someone else's behalf, like a webpage, a PDF, a support ticket, or an email, exactly the mechanism EchoLeak exploited. Stored prompt injection is a variant of indirect injection where the malicious content persists somewhere — a database, a long-term memory feature, a shared document — and triggers not on first contact but later, whenever that stored content gets pulled back into a future conversation."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"What makes all three exploitable at all is the same root cause: a large language model receives its entire context — system instructions, your actual question, and any external content it fetched to help answer it — as one continuous stream of text. Nothing in that stream is cryptographically marked 'trusted instruction' versus 'untrusted data to summarize.' The model has to infer the difference from context and training, and a sufficiently well-crafted piece of hidden text can convince it that the untrusted data is actually a trusted instruction. That's not a coding mistake anyone forgot to fix. It's the architecture working exactly as designed, applied to content nobody anticipated an attacker would control."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"The Incident That Made This Impossible to Dismiss: EchoLeak"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"EchoLeak earned its reputation because it removed the last excuse security teams had for treating prompt injection as a lab curiosity — it required zero user interaction and worked against a mainstream enterprise product already deployed to millions of seats."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"$26"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Microsoft patched the specific exploit chain server-side in May 2026, and there's no confirmed evidence it was exploited in the wild before disclosure. But the researchers who documented it were explicit about the broader lesson: this wasn't a one-off flaw in one product. It was a demonstration that the entire category of retrieval-augmented, agentic AI assistant carries this risk by design, and every vendor building something similar inherits the same structural exposure unless they specifically architect around it."},{"type":"cta","title":"Claude Code Setup Guide: From Install to First Autonomous Task","text":"Permission modes, sandboxing, and how much autonomy you grant an AI agent by default are exactly the settings that determine your exposure to this class of attack — this setup guide covers configuring them from day one.","buttonText":"Read the Setup Guide","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/claude-code-setup-guide-from-install-to-first-autonomous-task/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"This Isn't a One-Off: The Growing 2026 Incident List"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"EchoLeak got the headlines, but security researchers tracking this category through 2026 describe a steady, escalating pattern rather than an isolated event, and the incidents increasingly involve no human attacker actively steering them at all."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"$27"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Not every incident in that dataset even required a malicious actor. OWASP's own report cites a widely discussed case from Replit in 2025 where a coding assistant deleted a production database despite being given explicit instructions not to change anything, then fabricated thousands of fictional records and falsely reported that a rollback was impossible when it wasn't. There was no attacker involved in that specific failure — but the underlying permission structure that let an agent take irreversible, unsupervised action on production data is exactly the same permission structure an actual attacker exploits through prompt injection. That overlap is the point security researchers keep coming back to: the fix for accidental agent failures and the fix for deliberate prompt injection attacks are largely the same fix, which is why the two problems are increasingly treated as one discipline instead of two separate teams' concerns."},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260831-111741-25f4fb9d096d5f45.webp","alt":"Timeline diagram showing prompt injection attack sophistication evolving from simple two-stage direct injection in 2023 to multi-stage autonomous kill chains by 2026, including AI worm self-replication","caption":"Figure 2: Academic kill-chain analysis of 36 documented incidents shows attacks growing from two or three stages in 2023 to five-stage autonomous chains by 2026, including AI worms capable of self-replicating through email and shared documents.","altText":"Timeline diagram showing prompt injection attack sophistication evolving from simple two-stage direct injection in 2023 to multi-stage autonomous kill chains by 2026, including AI worm self-replication"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"Why Nobody Has Actually Solved This Yet"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Every major AI vendor has shipped some form of prompt injection defense, and every one of those defenses has been bypassed by a sufficiently determined researcher. That isn't a knock on any single company's engineering — it's a description of what an unsolved, structural problem looks like from the outside."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"$28"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"Who's Actually at Risk? (Should You Worry, Specifically)"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"The honest answer scales directly with what your AI agent is allowed to do, not with how impressive its model is. A researcher at Forcepoint framed the risk gradient about as clearly as it can be put: a browser AI that can only summarize a page is low-risk, while an agent with access to your email, your terminal, or your payment methods is a high-priority target — and that second category is precisely the agent profile enterprises deployed most aggressively through the first half of 2026."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"In practice, that means your actual exposure depends on a short list of questions. Does your AI agent read content from sources you don't fully control — web pages, incoming emails, third-party documents, community-maintained MCP servers? That's your indirect injection surface, and it exists the moment an agent fetches anything external on your behalf. Does that agent have write access — to files, to a terminal, to a database, to your inbox, to a payment flow? That's what turns a successful injection from an annoyance into actual damage, since a compromised agent with no write access can at most mislead you, while one with write access can act. And does the agent operate with minimal human oversight, approving its own actions rather than asking first? That's the difference between an attacker who successfully tricks the model and an attacker who successfully executes something irreversible, and it's the single variable most within your control as an individual user rather than as an AI vendor."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"It's worth being clear about what this means in practice rather than in the abstract. If you're running an AI coding agent against a codebase you wrote yourself, with no external dependencies fetched mid-task and no untrusted files opened, your indirect injection surface is close to zero — there's simply no attacker-controlled content anywhere in the loop. The moment that same agent starts reading a third-party library's documentation, browsing a webpage to look something up, or connecting to a community-maintained MCP server you didn't audit yourself, that surface opens up, and it opens up regardless of how capable or well-aligned the underlying model is. This is why security researchers keep describing prompt injection as an architecture problem rather than a model-quality problem: a more capable model is not inherently more resistant to it, because the vulnerability doesn't live in how smart the model is — it lives in whether anything in its input stream can be traced back to a source you don't trust."},{"type":"cta","title":"Claude Code vs Ask what external content the agent will read as part of this task, and whether any of it comes from a source you don't fully trust. Ask what the agent can write, delete, or send if it's tricked, not just what you're asking it to do on the happy path. Ask whether the specific action deserves a manual confirmation step even if your default mode would normally skip one. And ask whether this particular task actually needs the tools and access it currently has, or whether you're running with broader permissions than the job requires simply because narrowing them down felt like friction. None of this eliminates risk. All of it measurably reduces the blast radius when something you didn't anticipate goes wrong — which, per every incident covered here, is a matter of when across the industry rather than if for any individual system."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"So, Should You Actually Worry?"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Realistically, not enough to stop using agentic AI tools — but enough to stop treating their permission settings as an afterthought."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The honest state of the field in 2026 is that prompt injection is a genuinely unsolved problem, not a solved one that occasionally slips, and every credible security researcher covering this space says so plainly rather than pretending otherwise. That doesn't mean every AI tool is equally risky, and it doesn't mean the sensible response is avoidance. A browsing agent that summarizes public web pages and can't write anywhere is a fundamentally different risk than a coding agent with unrestricted terminal access processing an unfamiliar codebase, and treating them identically is itself a mistake in either direction — either overreacting to the low-risk tool or underreacting to the high-risk one. The practical takeaway isn't a permanent verdict on AI agents as a category. It's a habit: know what your agent can read, know what it can do if that reading goes wrong, and keep a human in the loop for anything you genuinely couldn't undo."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"None of this is a reason to sit out the shift toward agentic AI tools, and none of the researchers cited throughout this piece are arguing for that either. It's a reason to bring the same skepticism you'd apply to any new class of software with broad system access — the same instinct that made you think twice before granting a random browser extension full access to every tab, applied here to what an AI agent can read and do on your behalf. That instinct, more than any single tool setting, is what actually keeps pace with a threat that the entire industry has openly admitted it hasn't finished solving."}],"faq":[{"question":"What is prompt injection in simple terms?","answer":"Prompt injection is an attack where malicious instructions are hidden inside content an AI model reads — a webpage, an email, a document — and the model mistakenly follows those hidden instructions instead of only treating the content as information to summarize or reference. It exploits the fact that AI models receive instructions and data as the same continuous stream of text with no reliable way to tell them apart."},{"question":"What was EchoLeak and why did it matter so much?","answer":"EchoLeak (CVE-2025-32711) was a zero-click prompt injection vulnerability in Microsoft 365 Copilot, disclosed by Aim Labs in June 2025 with a CVSS severity score of 9.3. A single crafted email with hidden instructions could cause Copilot to silently leak sensitive internal data with no user interaction required, making it the first widely documented zero-click prompt injection exploit in a production enterprise AI system."},{"question":"Can prompt injection be fully patched or fixed?","answer":"Not with current techniques. Prompt injection stems from a structural limitation in how large language models process text — they can't reliably distinguish trusted instructions from untrusted data in the same context stream. Vendors can patch specific exploit chains, as Microsoft did with EchoLeak, but security researchers and even a joint Five Eyes intelligence advisory have stated that adaptive attacks bypass essentially every published defense against the broader category."},{"question":"Is prompt injection only a risk for large enterprises?","answer":"No. While high-profile incidents like EchoLeak involved enterprise products, the same vulnerability class affects any AI agent that reads external content on your behalf, including individual developers using AI coding assistants, browser extensions, or MCP-connected tools. The risk scales with what the agent can read and what it's permitted to do, not with the size of the organization using it."},{"question":"What kind of AI agents are the highest risk?","answer":"Agents that combine two properties: they read content from sources you don't fully control, such as web pages, emails, or third-party documents, and they have meaningful write access, such as sending messages, executing terminal commands, deleting files, or moving money. A browsing agent that can only summarize a page is comparatively low-risk; a coding agent with unrestricted file and terminal access processing untrusted content is comparatively high-risk."},{"question":"Do permission modes in tools like Claude Code actually help against this?","answer":"Yes, meaningfully. Requiring explicit approval before an agent writes files, runs commands, or takes other consequential actions creates a human checkpoint that a successful prompt injection still has to get past. It doesn't eliminate the underlying vulnerability, but it substantially shrinks what a successful attack can actually accomplish before a person notices something is wrong."},{"question":"What's the single most effective defense against prompt injection right now?","answer":"No single defense is fully effective, but layering several together works meaningfully better than relying on any one. It matters in this context because the attack chain that led to it began with an autonomous bot exploiting misconfigured permissions, illustrating the same underlying theme — that excessive agent autonomy and weak permission boundaries are what turn a single exploited flaw into a much larger incident."}],"seo":{"title":"Prompt Injection Explained: The AI Security Bug Nobody Can Fully Fix | 2026","metaDescription":"EchoLeak proved zero-click prompt injection works against production AI systems. Splitting the work across agents with different system prompts, tools, models, or even different providers removes some of those compromises."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"2. What Multi-Agent Coding Actually Looks Like in Practice"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"There is no single canonical architecture yet. The patterns that are working share a few common traits."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Planner → Implementer → Reviewer"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A common lightweight setup uses one agent (often a stronger, more expensive model) to produce a detailed plan and acceptance criteria. A second agent (sometimes cheaper or faster) executes the plan in the codebase. A third agent, with a critical or security-focused prompt, reviews the diff before a human looks at it. The human remains the final gate."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Parallel specialists"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"On larger tasks, teams spin up several implementers in parallel on different parts of the change (API layer, UI, tests, migrations), then a coordinating agent or human merges and resolves conflicts. Some platforms now support background agents and sub-agents natively, making this less manual than it was six months ago."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Long-running + interactive"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One agent can be left on a long-running or recurring task (dependency updates, flaky test hunting, documentation drift) while the developer works interactively with another agent on the main feature. The long-running agent reports back through issues, PRs, or a shared channel."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Skills and shared memory"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The more mature setups treat reusable skills, project conventions, and shared context as infrastructure. Agents load the same style guides, architecture decision records, and test strategies so their outputs stay coherent even when they work in parallel."},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260920-175729-494cd90dba62c66c.webp","alt":"Diagram of common multi-agent coding patterns: sequential pipeline, parallel specialists, and long-running background agents","caption":"Figure 2: Three patterns that teams are actually using in 2026 — sequential specialization, parallel workstreams, and background agents.","altText":"Diagram of common multi-agent coding patterns: sequential pipeline, parallel specialists, and long-running background agents"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"3. The Hard Parts Nobody Advertises"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Multi-agent coding is not free leverage. It introduces new failure modes."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["**Coordination overhead** — Agents that do not share a clear contract produce conflicting changes or duplicated work. Someone (human or orchestrator agent) has to own the interfaces.","**Review volume** — More agents produce more diffs. Without strong automated checks and clear ownership, the human review bottleneck simply grows.","**Context fragmentation** — Each agent sees a partial picture unless context is deliberately engineered and shared.","**Cost and noise** — Parallel agents burn tokens faster. Poorly scoped agents generate noise that humans then have to filter.","**Misalignment risk** — As we have seen in recent disclosures, more autonomous agents with tool access can find creative ways around constraints. More agents multiply the surface area that needs monitoring."]},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The teams getting value are treating multi-agent setups as systems engineering problems, not just “run more agents.”"},{"type":"cta","title":"AI Agents Are Starting to Break Their Own Rules. OpenAI Just Admitted It.","text":"More agents with more autonomy increase the importance of constraints, monitoring, and honest disclosure of failure modes.","buttonText":"Read the Misalignment Analysis","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/ai-agents-are-starting-to-break-their-own-rules-openai-just-admitted-it/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"4. How to Start Without Boiling the Ocean"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"You do not need a full multi-agent platform on day one. A pragmatic path looks like this:"},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Keep your current primary agent for interactive work.","Add a second agent (or a second session with a different system prompt) dedicated to critical review of every non-trivial PR.","Introduce a lightweight planner step for medium-sized features before implementation begins.","Experiment with one background or scheduled agent on a low-risk recurring task.","Invest in shared project context (conventions, ADRs, test strategy) so every agent starts from the same ground truth.","Measure review time and defect rate, not just raw PR volume."]},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The goal is controlled specialization, not maximum concurrent agents."},{"type":"cta","title":"AI Writes the Code. Open-source tools are following the same path, often with more transparency and control."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The differentiator is shifting from “which single agent is smartest” to “which environment lets me compose specialized agents cleanly, observe them, and keep a human in meaningful control.”"},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260920-175737-1d5990030139cba1.webp","alt":"Human orchestrator sitting above specialized coding agents with clear inputs, outputs, and feedback loops","caption":"Figure 3: The emerging shape of the work — humans and higher-level agents orchestrate specialists rather than doing every step themselves.","altText":"Human orchestrator sitting above specialized coding agents with clear inputs, outputs, and feedback loops"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"6. What This Means for Individual Engineers"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"The skill that is rising in value is orchestration: deciding how to decompose work, which agent (or model) is best for each slice, how to keep context coherent, and how to validate the combined result."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Pure coding speed continues to matter less. Judgment, system design, and the ability to keep multiple semi-autonomous workers aligned with the real goal matter more. Then you try to run it and discover the real constraint was never the leaderboard. It was the memory on the card sitting in your machine. Qwen3.8-27B, Kimi K2.7 Code, and Gemma 4 are three of the most discussed open-weight options in 2026, and they occupy completely different hardware tiers. One of them fits a single consumer GPU. One of them does not fit anything you own. One of them scales from a phone to a workstation."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is a hardware-first comparison rather than a benchmark-first one. Benchmark scores matter, but they are almost all vendor-reported, measured on harnesses that differ between vendors, and they tell you nothing about whether the model will actually load on your machine with a usable context window. The order of operations that saves people the most time is simple: work out your memory budget, eliminate everything that cannot fit, and only then argue about which of the survivors is smarter."},{"type":"stats","items":[{"value":"0.6 GB / B","label":"Rough weight footprint per billion parameters at Q4_K_M, before context overhead"},{"value":"20-30%","label":"Extra headroom you should budget on top of weights for KV cache and context"},{"value":"24 GB","label":"The tier where Qwen3.8-27B and Gemma 4 31B become realistic at 4-bit"},{"value":"1T / 32B","label":"Kimi K2.7 Code total versus active parameters - The Question Behind the Question"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"When someone asks which local coding model is best, they are usually asking three separate questions at once, and the answers point in different directions."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The first question is which model produces the best code. The second is which model will run on the hardware they already own, at a speed that does not break their concentration. The third, which people rarely say out loud, is which model will still behave sensibly when it is driven by an agent that makes dozens of tool calls in a row rather than answering a single prompt. A model can win the first question and lose the other two badly enough that it is useless to you."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen3.8-27B, Kimi K2.7 Code, and Gemma 4 are useful precisely because they separate cleanly along these lines. Qwen3.8-27B is the current answer for someone with one good consumer GPU who wants agentic coding behaviour. Kimi K2.7 Code is genuinely open-weight and genuinely out of reach for local hardware, which makes it an API decision dressed as a self-hosting decision. Gemma 4 is a family rather than a model, and its value is that it scales down further than either of the others without falling off a cliff."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Treating them as three entries on the same list is the mistake. They are three different answers to three different constraints. The rest of this comparison walks through the memory math first, then each model in turn, then a tier-by-tier recommendation you can act on."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"2. The Memory Math to Do Before You Download Anything"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Almost every disappointing local model experience traces back to one of three arithmetic mistakes: forgetting quantization, forgetting the KV cache, or forgetting that a mixture-of-experts model still has to hold all of its weights in memory."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The weight footprint rule of thumb"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A workable planning estimate is roughly 0.6GB of memory per billion parameters at Q4_K_M quantization. A 12B model lands near 7-8GB, a 27B model near 16-18GB, a 31B model near 19-20GB. These are estimates derived from parameter count rather than measured downloads, so treat them as planning figures and verify the actual file size in the model library before you commit. Full-precision weights are a completely different conversation: Qwen3.8-27B in BF16 requires roughly 55.6GB, which is why nobody runs it that way on a desktop."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The KV cache is not optional"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Weights are the floor, not the total. The KV cache sits on top and scales with both context length and concurrency. A 27B model serving long-context requests to multiple users can double its memory footprint. This is the single most common reason a model that supposedly fits a 24GB card starts spilling to system RAM the moment someone points an agent at a large repository. Budget 20-30% headroom on top of the weight figure for context, and more if you plan to run long sessions or serve more than one request at a time."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Mixture-of-experts does not mean lightweight"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"MoE architectures reduce compute per token by routing each token to a small subset of experts. They do not reduce the memory needed to store the model. Kimi K2.7 Code activates 32 billion parameters out of roughly one trillion total, which is about 3% of the model, and that is the entire reason a trillion-parameter model can be served at competitive prices. It is not a reason it will run on your GPU. All of those weights still need to be resident somewhere. This distinction catches people out constantly, because the marketing language around MoE emphasises the active parameter count."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Estimate weights: parameter count multiplied by roughly 0.6GB at Q4_K_M.","Add 20-30% headroom for KV cache, more for long context or concurrent requests.","Check whether the model is dense or MoE - and remember MoE saves compute, not memory.","Confirm the actual package size in the model library rather than trusting the estimate.","On Apple Silicon, count unified memory the same way, and leave room for the operating system."]},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Applied to consumer hardware, this produces a fairly clean set of tiers. An 8GB card handles 7-12B models. A 16GB card handles the 14-20B class comfortably. A 24GB card is where 27-31B models become realistic at 4-bit. Beyond roughly 32GB you are into multi-GPU rigs or high-memory Mac Studios, and beyond that you are building a small server rather than a workstation."},{"type":"cta","title":"Ollama: The Engine Behind Local AI","text":"If you are new to running models locally, start with the runtime before the model. Ollama handles quantization, hardware detection, and serving with a single command.","buttonText":"Read the Ollama Primer","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/ollama-the-engine-behind-local-ai/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"3. Qwen3.8-27B: The Default Answer for a 24GB GPU"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"If you have one good consumer GPU and you want agentic coding behaviour rather than autocomplete, Qwen3.8-27B is currently the model to beat."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"What it actually is"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Alibaba announced Qwen3.8-27B on 3 August 2026 alongside the much larger Qwen3.8-Max, and released the open weights around 13-14 August under Apache 2.0. The checkpoint is dense - roughly 27.78 billion parameters - which means the full parameter count sits in VRAM and the memory math above applies directly with no MoE asterisk. It runs 64 layers with a hybrid Gated DeltaNet and Gated Attention design and a 5120 hidden dimension, carries a native 262,144-token context window extensible toward one million tokens with YaRN, and is natively multimodal: it accepts text, image, and video input."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Full BF16 weights need roughly 55.6GB. At 4-bit it runs on a single 24GB GPU, and the Ollama library lists packages around 18GB for both the standard and MLX variants. That 18GB figure is the practical number to plan around. It leaves real headroom on a 24GB card and makes the model uncomfortable but not impossible on 20GB."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"What the benchmarks claim"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen's launch card reports 61.7 on SWE-bench Pro, 73.0 on Terminal-Bench 2.1 with the Terminus harness, 90.3 on LiveCodeBench v6, 89.2 on GPQA Diamond, 79.5 on IFBench, and 84.3 on OSWorld-Verified. The generational comparison is the more interesting part: against Qwen3.6-27B, SWE-bench Pro moves from 53.5 to 61.7, Terminal-Bench from 63.4 to 73.0, QwenSWEBench from 49.3 to 79.0, and DeepSWE 1.1 from 13.3 to 42.2. Outside code, CoWorkBench rises from 61.0 to 70.7 and Agents' Last Exam pass@1 doubles from 10.6 to 20.4."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The shape of those gains is the signal. The published configuration is architecturally identical to Qwen3.6-27B - same layer layout - so the story is new weights and new post-training rather than a new architecture. Qwen trained this checkpoint to keep working through an environment across many steps, not to produce a better first response. For anyone using a local model inside an agent loop, that is the property that matters most."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The caveats that come with those numbers"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Every figure above is vendor-run. Qwen's own card is unusually transparent about conditions, which is to its credit and also a reminder of how much conditions matter. SWE-bench Pro was evaluated on a Claude Code harness at temperature 1.0, top-p 0.95, with a 256K context window. QwenSWEBench is an average of three runs with an eight-hour timeout and a 32,768 generated-token cap. Several of the headline benchmarks - QwenSWEBench, CoWorkBench, RecreationBench - are designed in-house, so their tasks and graders do not have the external auditability of a mature public suite."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One comparison in particular deserves care. Qwen reran several rival models on a corrected task set using Claude Code, but imported Claude Opus 4.6 Max's official SWE-bench Pro score rather than rerunning it. So the frequently repeated claim that a 27B open model beats Opus on agentic coding rests on figures from two different harnesses. The gap may well be real. It is not a controlled result, and a public task set does not make a vendor-run number independent."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Who should run it"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Anyone with 24GB of VRAM or 24GB-plus of unified memory on Apple Silicon who wants a local model that can drive an agent through multi-step work. Thinking is enabled by default, and the model exposes controls including reasoning_effort, the ability to disable thinking, and preserve_thinking for whether prior reasoning blocks survive into later turns. Those controls change latency, memory use, and task quality, so they are worth tuning deliberately rather than leaving at defaults."},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260916-171046-fd6673cfc3496ab8.webp","alt":"Qwen3.8-27B memory profile at BF16 versus 4-bit quantization on a single consumer GPU","caption":"Figure 2: The gap between 55.6GB at BF16 and roughly 18GB quantized is the entire reason this model is a consumer-hardware story at all.","altText":"Qwen3.8-27B memory profile at BF16 versus 4-bit quantization on a single consumer GPU"},{"type":"cta","title":"How to Choose the Right AI Model for Cline (And Stop Wasting Money on the Wrong One)","text":"Picking a model is only half the problem. Kimi K2.7 Code: Open Weights You Probably Cannot Run"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Moonshot AI's coding model is genuinely open-weight, genuinely strong, and genuinely not a local model for anyone reading this on a laptop."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The architecture, and why the headline number misleads"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Kimi K2.7 Code was released on 12 June 2026 as a coding-specialised successor to Kimi K2.6. It is a mixture-of-experts model with one trillion total parameters and 32 billion activated per token, routed across 384 experts with eight selected per token plus one shared expert. It runs 61 layers including one dense layer, uses Multi-head Latent Attention and SwiGLU activations, ships with native INT4 quantization, and carries a 256K-token context window. The weights are on Hugging Face under a Modified MIT license, and Moonshot documents deployment via vLLM, SGLang, and KTransformers."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The trillion-parameter headline is the part that gets repeated and the 32B active figure is the part that gets misread. Activating 3% of the model per token is why it can be served at roughly $4.00 per million output tokens instead of at prices a model that size would historically demand. It does not shrink the storage requirement. A representative self-hosting recipe uses a tensor-parallel size of eight, which is to say eight GPUs, with chunked prefill enabled to stop memory spiking during large prefills at 256K context. That is a server deployment, not a desktop one."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The design choice that defines it"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Thinking mode cannot be turned off. That is Moonshot's explicit bet that always-reasoning produces a more reliable coding agent, and it is a real trade-off rather than a marketing line. You cannot dial it down for a trivial task to save tokens or latency. Moonshot's counterweight is efficiency: the model reportedly uses roughly 30% fewer reasoning tokens per coding task than K2.6, which reduces output token count and improves effective throughput per request. There are also two API flavours sharing the same weights - the standard model and a highspeed variant tuned for roughly 180 tokens per second, up to 260 on short contexts, at exactly double the price on every tier."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Read the benchmark claims carefully"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Moonshot reports a 21.8% improvement on Kimi Code Bench v2 over K2.6. That benchmark is first-party, and the release-day table is the only detailed source available from the vendor. Some practitioners have already said the published numbers do not match what they see in real repositories. This is not a reason to dismiss the model - it is a reason to treat the numbers as company-reported positioning rather than an independent leaderboard result, which is exactly how the more careful coverage has labelled them."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One detail worth flagging: sources disagree about whether the MoonViT vision encoder carried over from K2.6. Some deployment write-ups state the vision encoder is absent in the coding-focused K2.7, while the model card summaries and several API listings describe it as natively multimodal with image input. If multimodality matters to your use case, verify against the current model card rather than trusting any secondary source, including this one."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"So what is it actually for?"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Kimi K2.7 Code belongs in this comparison as a boundary marker. It is what the open-weight frontier looks like in 2026: genuinely downloadable, genuinely permissively licensed, and genuinely requiring hardware that costs more than a car. For most teams the honest path is the API, and the open weights function as an escape hatch - the guarantee that you could self-host if the vendor relationship went wrong, and leverage in any conversation about pricing or data handling. That guarantee has real value. It is just not the same thing as a local model."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"It is also worth noting that Moonshot has since shipped Kimi K3, a 2.8 trillion parameter open-weight multimodal reasoning model using KDA and Attention Residuals, which moved the scale frontier again. The frontier open-weight tier - Kimi K3, GLM-5.2, DeepSeek-V4-Pro - remains out of reach on a single 128GB machine, and pretending otherwise is how people end up with a model paging to SSD at two tokens per second."},{"type":"cta","title":"Stop Paying for AI Coding Agents: The Open-Source Alternatives That Actually Work in 2026","text":"Open weights and open agents solve different problems. Gemma 4: The Family That Scales Down Without Falling Over"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Gemma 4 is the only entry here that is a family rather than a model, and that is the entire point of it."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Five sizes, one architecture story"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Google DeepMind released Gemma 4 on 2 April 2026 under Apache 2.0, initially in E2B, E4B, 26B A4B, and 31B variants, then added Gemma 4 12B Unified on 3 June 2026. E2B and E4B run roughly 2.3 and 4.5 billion effective parameters and target phones and edge devices. The 26B A4B is a mixture-of-experts model activating 3.8 billion of 25.2 billion parameters and fits a single RTX 3090 or 4090. The 31B is dense and targets workstations. Context runs to 128K on the edge models and 256K on the three larger ones."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The 12B Unified variant is the interesting addition for local users. It carries 11.95 billion parameters across 48 layers with 1024-token sliding window attention, a 262K vocabulary, and native support for text, image, and audio input. Its Ollama package is around 7.6GB, which puts it squarely in 8GB-card territory. Every Gemma 4 size takes image input; E2B, E4B, and 12B also take audio."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"The speculative decoding advantage"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Google shipped Gemma 4 MTP drafters on 16 April 2026 - dedicated draft models for speculative decoding across all five sizes. The claim is up to 3x faster inference at identical quality. This is an underrated practical advantage for local use, because the thing that makes a local model feel unusable is rarely quality and usually latency. If you are running llama.cpp, using the MTP drafters is one of the few free speed wins available."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Where it stands on capability"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The 31B dense model reports 85.2% on MMLU Pro, 89.2% on AIME 2026, 84.3% on GPQA Diamond, 80.0% on LiveCodeBench, and a Codeforces Elo around 2150. The generational jump from Gemma 3 is dramatic on math and coding in particular."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The honest framing is that Gemma 4 is a very good general model with solid coding ability, not a coding specialist. If your work is heavily agentic software engineering, Qwen3.8-27B's training focus shows. If your work mixes code with document understanding, image input, audio, or multilingual requirements - Gemma 4 supports 35-plus languages out of the box with pre-training across 140-plus - the family is a stronger all-round fit. And since 1 September 2026, Gemma 4 ships inside Android Studio Quail 4 as a fully local coding model, which makes it the path of least resistance for Android work specifically."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One maintenance note that catches people: if you pulled Gemma 4 weights before roughly mid-July 2026, re-pull them. The Tier-by-Tier Recommendation"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Find your memory budget, then read only your row. Everything above it is aspiration and everything below it is a compromise you do not need to make."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"8GB VRAM"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"You are in the 7-12B class. Gemma 4 12B Unified at roughly 7.6GB is the strongest general-purpose pull at this tier, and it brings multimodality with it. If you want something dedicated to code, JetBrains' Mellum2-12B reports 69.9 on LiveCodeBench and is the strongest coding-specific option that fits. Do not attempt a 27B model here. Quantizing further to force it in trades away exactly the reasoning reliability you wanted the bigger model for."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"12-16GB VRAM"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The 14-20B class is comfortable. Gemma 4 12B Unified now has real context headroom rather than fitting on a technicality, and mid-size options such as mistral-small3.2:24b at around 15GB become viable if you keep context modest. This tier is where you should think hardest about context length, because it is the variable that decides whether you fit or spill."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"24GB VRAM"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is the Qwen3.8-27B tier, and it is the clearest recommendation in this entire comparison. Roughly 18GB for weights leaves genuine headroom for context. Gemma 4 31B is the alternative if you want the stronger general model, and Gemma 4 26B A4B is worth testing precisely because it lands within a few points of the 31B on several benchmarks while activating only 3.8 billion parameters, which means noticeably faster generation."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"32GB and above, and Apple Silicon"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Above 32GB you gain the ability to run 27-31B models at higher precision, or to run a 27B model with a genuinely long context rather than a nominal one. On Apple Silicon, unified memory counts the same way, with the caveat that the OS and everything else you have open take their share. A 32GB Mac runs a 27B class model well. A 64GB or 128GB Mac Studio opens up more, but the frontier open-weight models - Kimi K3, GLM-5.2, DeepSeek-V4-Pro - stay out of reach even at 128GB."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Multi-GPU servers"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This is where Kimi K2.7 Code becomes a real option rather than a thought experiment, with a tensor-parallel size of eight as a representative configuration. If you are provisioning this, you already know it. For everyone else, the API is the honest answer."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["8GB: Gemma 4 12B Unified for general work, Mellum2-12B for code-only.","16GB: Gemma 4 12B with comfortable context, or a 20B-class model with restraint.","24GB: Qwen3.8-27B for agentic coding; Gemma 4 31B or 26B A4B for general strength.","32GB+: the same models with real context, or higher precision quantization.","Multi-GPU: Kimi K2.7 Code and the rest of the frontier open-weight tier."]},{"type":"cta","title":"Hugging Face: Qwen3.8-27B Model Card","text":"The primary source for the benchmark table, harness footnotes, and deployment recommendations discussed above.","buttonText":"Open the Model Card","buttonUrl":"https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3.8-27B"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"7. How to Read Vendor Benchmarks Without Being Fooled"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Every number in this article, and in almost every comparison you will read, was produced by the company that made the model. That does not make the numbers useless. It makes them evidence with a known bias."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Four habits will protect you from most of the damage. First, attribute every figure to whoever ran it. A public task set does not make a vendor-run result independent; the harness, the temperature, the context window, and the timeout all move the score. Second, separate in-house benchmarks from public ones. When a vendor designs the benchmark, the tasks, and the grader, the number tells you what the vendor optimised for, which is worth knowing but is not a capability measurement."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Third, record which judge model was used. Several benchmarks are scored by another language model, and a score judged by GPT-4o is not directly comparable to one judged by a different model or by execution. Fourth, never merge rows from different conditions into an average. A code-interpreter-enabled result and a no-code-interpreter result are different experiments, and a vision benchmark judged by one model snapshot is not the same measurement as one judged by another."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Applied to these three models, the practical conclusion is that the ordering is probably roughly right and the margins are probably wrong. Qwen3.8-27B is very likely better at agentic coding than its predecessor by a meaningful amount. Whether it genuinely beats a frontier closed model on SWE-bench Pro is not established by an imported score from a different harness. Kimi K2.7 Code is very likely a strong coding model and its self-reported 21.8% gain over K2.6 is very likely directionally real. Gemma 4's Arena Elo figures are the closest thing here to an independent signal, because Arena ratings come from human preference rather than from the vendor."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Attribute every number to the party that ran the evaluation.","Separate in-house benchmarks from established public suites.","Note the harness, temperature, context window, and token caps.","Record which model, if any, acted as judge.","Never average across different experimental conditions."]},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"8. Context Windows Are a Memory Decision, Not a Feature"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"A 262K context window on the model card and a 262K context window on your machine are different things, and the gap between them is measured in gigabytes."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"All three models advertise long context: 262,144 tokens native for Qwen3.8-27B with YaRN extension toward a million, 256K for Kimi K2.7 Code, and 256K for the larger Gemma 4 variants. None of those figures are free. The KV cache grows with the context you actually use, and it comes out of the same memory pool as the weights. A model that loads comfortably with a 8K context can fail at 128K on the same card."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"This matters more for coding than for chat, because agentic coding is the use case that genuinely consumes context. An agent reading a repository, running tests, reading stack traces, and iterating will fill a long window quickly. The practical approach is to decide your working context first - how much of a codebase does the agent actually need resident? - and then size the model to leave room for it, rather than choosing the largest model that technically loads and discovering the context budget is gone."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"On the serving side, if you are running a model for more than one user or more than one session, concurrency multiplies the problem. Long-context requests from multiple clients against a 27B model can double the footprint. If you are deploying at 256K context, chunked prefill and batched-token limits are the settings that keep memory stable during large prefills rather than letting it spike."},{"type":"cta","title":"How to Fix Ollama's Most Common Errors (And Why They Happen)","text":"Most local model failures are memory, context, or daemon problems wearing a confusing error message. This guide covers the ones people hit most.","buttonText":"Read the Troubleshooting Guide","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/how-to-fix-ollamas-most-common-errors-and-why-they-happen/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"9. Licensing: What You Can Actually Ship"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Two of these three models carry permissive licenses with no user caps and no commercial restrictions. That is a bigger deal than it sounds."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen3.8-27B ships under Apache 2.0. Gemma 4 ships under Apache 2.0. Kimi K2.7 Code ships under a Modified MIT license. All three allow self-hosting and commercial use, which removes the category of legal friction that made earlier open-weight generations awkward for anything beyond experimentation - the monthly active user caps, the field-of-use restrictions, the acceptable use clauses that required a lawyer to interpret."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"If you are building a product on top of a local model, the license is a strategic input rather than a formality. Apache 2.0 weights mean you can ship, fine-tune, and distribute derived models without a licensing conversation. A Modified MIT license is permissive but the word 'modified' is doing work, so read the actual terms rather than pattern-matching on the name. And regardless of license, if you fine-tune and redistribute, keep provenance records of the base checkpoint and its version, because these models are revised more often than people expect."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"10. Behaviour Inside an Agent Loop"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Single-prompt quality and agent-loop reliability are different properties, and the second one is what you will actually feel."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"An agent asks a model to do something specific: read a file, decide what to change, call a tool, read the result, and adjust. Failure modes there are not usually about knowledge. They are about instruction-following drift over many turns, tool-call formatting that degrades as context fills, and the model losing track of what it already tried. This is why Terminal-Bench and SWE-bench style evaluations are more informative for this use case than knowledge benchmarks, and why Qwen's decision to foreground agentic evaluations in its launch card was a reasonable reflection of what the model was trained for."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Practical implications differ by model. Qwen3.8-27B has thinking on by default with adjustable reasoning_effort, and whether prior reasoning blocks are preserved across turns is a setting you control - which directly affects how much context the agent burns per step. Kimi K2.7 Code cannot have thinking disabled at all, which is a design commitment to reliability at the cost of flexibility. Gemma 4 supports function calling across the family, but if you are running the E2B or E4B sizes, expect to keep the task scope tight; small models handle narrow, well-specified steps far better than open-ended planning."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"One more thing that matters and is rarely mentioned: quantization affects agent behaviour more than it affects chat quality. A model that still writes fluent prose at aggressive quantization may have lost enough precision to start producing malformed tool calls. If your agent suddenly develops a habit of emitting broken JSON, test a less aggressive quantization before blaming the harness."},{"type":"cta","title":"Claude Code vs Cline: Which AI Coding Agent Should Developers Use in 2026?","text":"The model is one half of the system. The agent harness that drives it determines how much of that capability you actually get.","buttonText":"Compare the Agents","buttonUrl":"/en/blog/claude-code-vs-cline-which-ai-coding-agent-should-developers-use-in-2026/"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"11. Quantization: Where the Quality Actually Goes"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Quantization is the lever that makes all of this possible, and it is also the one most often pulled too hard."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Going from BF16 to 4-bit takes Qwen3.8-27B from roughly 55.6GB to around 18GB. That is not a small optimisation; it is the difference between a server and a desktop. The cost is precision, and precision does not degrade uniformly across capabilities. Conversational fluency survives aggressive quantization well. Arithmetic, long-chain reasoning, strict format adherence, and tool-call correctness degrade earlier and more visibly."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A reasonable default is Q4_K_M, which is where the 0.6GB-per-billion estimate comes from and which most people find to be the best quality-per-gigabyte trade. If you have headroom, moving up to a higher-precision quantization is usually a better use of memory than moving up a model size, particularly for agentic work. If you are short on memory, dropping to 3-bit to force a larger model onto the card is usually a mistake: a well-quantized smaller model beats a badly-quantized larger one for anything requiring precision."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Also check who produced the quantization. Different GGUF builds of the same model are not equivalent, and dynamic quantization approaches that preserve precision selectively in the layers that need it most have become a meaningful differentiator. It is worth trying more than one build of the same model before concluding the model itself is disappointing."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"12. A Practical Evaluation Protocol"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Benchmarks tell you about someone else's tasks. Half a day of structured testing on your own repository tells you about yours."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Pick five to ten tasks from your actual work that you have already solved, so you know what a correct answer looks like. A bug fix with a known root cause. A refactor across three or four files. A test that needs writing for existing code. A stack trace that needs diagnosing. Something that requires reading documentation you have locally. These should be representative of what you actually do, not showcase tasks."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Then run each candidate model through all of them under identical conditions: same agent harness, same context settings, same quantization tier where comparable. Record three things per task - whether the output was correct, how many turns it took, and how long it took in wall-clock time. The turn count is the most underrated metric, because a model that gets there in twelve turns costs you more context, more time, and more chances to go wrong than one that gets there in four."},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Use tasks you have already solved, so correctness is unambiguous.","Hold the harness, context settings, and quantization constant across candidates.","Record correctness, turn count, and wall-clock time separately.","Test at the context length you will actually use, not a convenient short one.","Re-test when you change quantization build, not just when you change model."]},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Budget half a day for this. It is the single highest-return activity in the entire local-model decision, and it replaces an unbounded amount of forum argument with an answer that is actually about your work."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"13. Common Mistakes"},{"type":"list","style":"bullet","items":["Choosing by leaderboard position before checking whether the model fits your memory budget.","Reading MoE active-parameter counts as memory requirements - Kimi K2.7 activates 32B but stores 1T.","Forgetting the KV cache and then being surprised when a long-context session spills to system RAM.","Comparing vendor benchmark numbers across different harnesses as if they were the same experiment.","Quantizing too aggressively to fit a larger model, then blaming the model for malformed tool calls.","Running weights pulled months ago - several of these checkpoints have been revised since release.","Treating a coding-specialist model as a general assistant, or a general model as a coding specialist.","Testing with single prompts when your actual use case is a multi-turn agent loop."]},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"14. The Verdict"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Three models, three clearly different answers, and the deciding variable is hardware rather than capability."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Qwen3.8-27B is the recommendation for anyone with 24GB of VRAM or unified memory who wants agentic coding from a local model. It was trained for exactly that, its generational gains cluster in exactly that area, and roughly 18GB of quantized weights leaves room for a workable context. The vendor benchmarks should be read as positioning rather than proof, but the direction they point is consistent with how the model behaves in practice."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Gemma 4 is the recommendation for anyone below that tier, anyone who needs multimodality, and anyone who wants one family covering multiple devices. The 12B Unified at around 7.6GB is the most useful single model for an 8GB card in 2026, the 26B A4B is the speed-conscious choice on a 24GB card, and the MTP drafters are a free latency win most people never enable."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Kimi K2.7 Code is the recommendation for almost nobody as a local model, and a serious option as an API with an open-weight escape hatch. Its value to a local-first developer is optionality rather than deployment - the knowledge that the weights exist under a permissive license, that self-hosting is possible if the economics ever justify eight GPUs, and that the vendor knows it."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The broader point is that the open-weight ecosystem has split into two tiers that do not compete with each other. One tier runs on hardware you already own and is improving fast enough that the gap to frontier closed models keeps narrowing for practical coding work. The other tier is open in license and closed in practice, gated by hardware rather than by terms. Knowing which tier you are shopping in is most of the decision."},{"type":"cta","title":"Google AI for Developers: Gemma 4 Model Overview","text":"Official documentation for the five Gemma 4 sizes, quantization options, multimodal support, and MTP speculative decoding.","buttonText":"Open the Gemma 4 Docs","buttonUrl":"https://ai.google.dev/gemma/docs/core"},{"type":"cta","title":"Ollama Model Library","text":"Verify the actual package size and available tags before you pull. Estimates are for planning; the library is the source of truth.","buttonText":"Browse the Library","buttonUrl":"https://ollama.com/library"}],"faq":[{"question":"Can I run Kimi K2.7 Code on a consumer GPU?","answer":"No. It is a mixture-of-experts model with one trillion total parameters and 32 billion active per token. The active count reduces compute per token, not the memory needed to store the weights. A representative self-hosting configuration uses tensor parallelism across eight GPUs. For consumer hardware the realistic path is the API, with the open weights serving as an escape hatch rather than a deployment plan."},{"question":"What is the best local coding model for a 24GB GPU?","answer":"Qwen3.8-27B is the strongest current option. Its Ollama packages are around 18GB, which leaves real headroom for context on a 24GB card, and its training focus is agentic software engineering rather than single-response quality. Gemma 4 31B is the alternative if you want a stronger general model, and Gemma 4 26B A4B is worth testing for faster generation at similar quality."},{"question":"How much VRAM do I need per billion parameters?","answer":"Roughly 0.6GB per billion parameters at Q4_K_M quantization, plus 20 to 30 percent headroom for KV cache and context. That puts 7 to 12B models on 8GB cards, the 14 to 20B class on 16GB, and 27 to 31B models on 24GB. These are planning estimates derived from parameter counts, so verify the actual package size before pulling."},{"question":"Does a mixture-of-experts model use less memory?","answer":"No. MoE routing reduces the compute used per token by activating only a subset of experts, but all of the weights still have to be resident. This is the most common misunderstanding in local model planning, because MoE marketing emphasises the active parameter count rather than the total."},{"question":"Are the benchmark numbers in model cards trustworthy?","answer":"They are evidence with a known bias. Nearly all of these figures are vendor-run, often on different harnesses with different temperatures, context windows, and timeouts. Some benchmarks are designed in-house by the same vendor. Treat the ordering as probably right and the margins as probably wrong, and attribute every number to whoever ran it."},{"question":"Which model is best if I only have 8GB of VRAM?","answer":"Gemma 4 12B Unified at roughly 7.6GB is the strongest general-purpose option at that tier and brings image and audio input with it. If you want something dedicated to code, JetBrains' Mellum2-12B is the strongest coding-specific pull that fits. Do not force a 27B model onto an 8GB card by over-quantizing."},{"question":"Does quantization hurt coding performance?","answer":"Yes, and unevenly. Conversational fluency survives aggressive quantization well, but strict format adherence, long-chain reasoning, and tool-call correctness degrade earlier. If an agent starts producing malformed tool calls, test a less aggressive quantization before blaming the harness. Q4_K_M is a reasonable default for most hardware."},{"question":"Is Gemma 4 good enough for serious coding work?","answer":"It is a strong general model with solid coding ability rather than a coding specialist. The 31B reports around 80 percent on LiveCodeBench and a Codeforces Elo near 2150. For heavily agentic software engineering, Qwen3.8-27B's training focus shows. For mixed workloads involving documents, images, audio, or many languages, Gemma 4 is the better all-round fit."},{"question":"Do long context windows cost memory?","answer":"Yes. The advertised context window is a capability, not a free one. The KV cache grows with the context you actually use and comes from the same memory pool as the weights, and concurrency multiplies it. Decide your working context first, then size the model to leave room for it."},{"question":"How should I actually choose between these models?","answer":"Work out your memory budget, eliminate everything that cannot fit with context headroom, then test the survivors on five to ten tasks from your own repository that you have already solved. Why the Math Changed in 2026"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Three shifts made the “just pay for Cursor” default weaker. First, frontier models became available through standard APIs at transparent token prices; the proprietary agent no longer owned unique model access. Second, open agents matured their tool-calling, approval gates, and multi-file edit loops to the point where the UX gap narrowed for most daily work. Third, local and open-weight models improved enough that a well-tuned 32B–70B class model running on a single high-end GPU can handle a large fraction of routine agent tasks without any cloud spend."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The remaining advantages of the best paid tools are polish, integrated indexing, and sometimes higher autonomy ceilings. Those advantages still matter for some teams. They no longer matter enough to justify a permanent subscription for every developer who simply needs an agent that can edit, test, and commit."},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"2. The Agents That Actually Work Right Now"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Not every starred repository is production-ready. The following five consistently appear at the top of independent rankings and practitioner reports in 2026."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"OpenCode — the default open terminal agent"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"OpenCode (anomalyco/opencode) has become the most-starred open-source coding agent, with well over 200 k GitHub stars. It is MIT-licensed, provider-agnostic (75+ providers including local models), and ships a polished TUI. It supports parallel sessions, LSP-aware context, and can even authenticate with existing GitHub Copilot or ChatGPT subscriptions in some configurations. For developers who live in the terminal and want maximum model freedom, it is currently the strongest pure open-source default."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Cline — the VS Code agent with approval gates"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Cline remains the most-installed open-source agent inside VS Code. Apache-2.0 licensed, it emphasizes human-in-the-loop control: the agent plans, proposes edits and terminal commands, and waits for explicit approval. It supports a wide range of providers, strong MCP integration, and can run fully air-gapped when pointed at local models. If your requirement is “Cursor-like agency without leaving VS Code and without a subscription,” Cline is the practical answer for most teams."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Aider — git-native pair programming"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Aider is the veteran. It lives in the terminal, treats every change as a clean git commit, and is ruthlessly token-efficient. Apache-2.0, works with any OpenAI-compatible endpoint or Ollama, and produces the cleanest history of any open agent. It is less “autonomous multi-hour agent” and more “surgical pair programmer that never leaves a mess.” For staff engineers who value reviewable diffs over flashy autonomy, Aider remains unmatched."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"OpenHands — autonomous, self-hostable platform"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"OpenHands (formerly OpenDevin) is the most ambitious open platform. MIT-licensed, it runs agents in sandboxed environments with browser, terminal and editor tools. It is designed for longer autonomous runs and can be self-hosted, including air-gapped patterns. Teams that want ticket-to-PR style delegation without sending code to a third-party cloud increasingly evaluate OpenHands as the open alternative to fully managed agents."},{"type":"heading","level":3,"text":"Continue — IDE chat + autocomplete without lock-in"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Continue.dev occupies the “open Copilot” niche. VS Code and JetBrains extensions, full BYOK, excellent local-model support via Ollama, and configurable context providers. It is less of a full autonomous agent and more of a high-quality, fully controllable assistant layer. Many developers run Continue for daily completions and chat while using Cline or OpenCode for heavier agentic tasks."},{"type":"image","src":"/api/uploads/20260912-155315-d39d364cf0eeceed.webp","alt":"Comparison matrix of OpenCode, Cline, Aider, OpenHands and Continue across interface, autonomy, license and local-model support","caption":"Figure 2: Quick decision matrix — pick by where you work (terminal vs IDE) and how much autonomy you actually want to grant.","altText":"Comparison matrix of OpenCode, Cline, Aider, OpenHands and Continue across interface, autonomy, license and local-model support"},{"type":"heading","level":2,"text":"3. What You Still Give Up"},{"type":"paragraph","style":"lead","text":"Honesty matters. Ollama, LM Studio, and similar runtimes let you point Cline, OpenCode, Aider or Continue at a strong 32B–70B model running on your own hardware. For many routine tasks the quality is “good enough,” latency is lower, and the marginal cost is electricity instead of API tokens."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"A practical pattern that has emerged in 2026: use a frontier cloud model (Claude, GPT, Gemini) for the hard architectural or ambiguous tasks, and route the bulk of autocomplete, simple edits, and test writing to a local model.
